I sit there and worry about all of it. The practice, the patients, the retirement. And honestly, the worry feels completely out of proportion, even to me. I know I shouldn't be this worked up, but I just can't seem to shut it off.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling sad or down most of the time lately?\n\nPatient: Sad? No, I wouldn't say sad. I mean, I don't feel down in the dumps or anything like that. It's more just this... this constant motor running in my head. Helen actually asked me the same thing the other day. She was out in the driveway polishing the Airstream—she found this special aluminum polish online, comes in these little blue tins from Germany, I think. Supposedly it's what they use on vintage airplanes. She spent three hours out there, just buffing away. She's got incredible stamina for that sort of thing. Her brother, Arthur, is the same way. He rebuilds antique grandfather clocks. You wouldn't believe the intricate little brass gears in those things, they have to be calibrated perfectly or the whole chime sequence gets thrown off by a half-second. \n\nBut anyway, no, I'm not depressed or crying or anything. Just... wound up tight. All the time.\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced any sudden episodes of intense fear or discomfort?\n\nPatient: Sudden intense fear? Like a panic attack? No, nothing like that. I don't get those sudden bursts where my heart is pounding out of my chest or anything. It's more of a slow burn, you know? Like a pot left on the stove too long. Speaking of stoves, Helen wants to completely redo the galley kitchen in that Airstream. She's been looking at these tiny little propane ranges. They're adorable, really, but I keep telling her we need to make sure the ventilation is up to code. You can't just go trapping carbon monoxide in an aluminum tube. I had a patient once, Mr. Henderson, who nearly passed out in his garage because he left a generator running. He came in for a root canal the next week and told me the whole story. Fascinating guy, Mr. Henderson. He used to breed those champion show dogs... Afghan Hounds, I think? The ones with the long, silky hair. Takes hours to groom them. \n\nBut, uh, no. No sudden attacks of fear. Just the constant, simmering worry.\n\nDoctor: Is your worry spread across many different things in your life or is it mainly focused on one or two specific areas?\n\nPatient: It's really spread across... well, all sorts of things. Like I was saying earlier, my mind just keeps circling around everything. It's definitely not just one or two areas. \n\nTake the practice handoff, for instance, and whether it's financially sound. I was actually talking to my accountant, Jerry, about the transition paperwork just the other day. Jerry's been doing my books for thirty years. He's a real character—he actually breeds racing pigeons up on his roof. Can you imagine? The city council tried to fine him a few years back because of some zoning ordinance about livestock, but he fought it tooth and nail. He brought in this huge binder of historical precedents about carrier pigeons during World War II to the town hall. The homing instinct on those birds is just incredible. They use the Earth's magnetic field to navigate, you know. I watched a documentary on it once with Helen. \n\nBut, um... what was I saying? Right, the worrying. Yes, it's a bit of everything. It's the finances, it's whether my old patients are really being looked after properly by the new kid, it's this constant dread about whether I’ll just become completely useless once I’m fully retired next month. It just jumps from one thing to the next. And the frustrating part is, the worry feels completely out of proportion even to me. I know it doesn't make sense to fret this much, but my brain just won't let it go.\n\nDoctor: Is your worrying affecting your ability to do the things you need to do or enjoy on a daily basis?\n\nPatient: Well, yes, it... it is getting in the way. I mean, I’ve had to actually slow down and postpone parts of the retirement handoff. I just keep second-guessing every little decision, and I end up losing so much time at the clinic. It's frustrating. Even my hobbies aren't the same. I used to love painting and gardening, but they just don't feel as manageable anymore. \n\nSpeaking of gardening, Helen ordered these new heirloom tomato seeds from a catalog in Vermont. They have this incredible variety called a Brandywine, supposedly dates back to the 1880s. The catalog itself was fascinating, printed on this thick, recycled paper with beautiful botanical illustrations. The artist who drew them actually lives in a cabin off the grid in Maine and uses pigments she makes herself from foraging. She grinds up blueberries and certain types of bark... it's amazing what you can do with natural materials. I tried to mix my own oil paints once, years ago, but getting the consistency right with the linseed oil is incredibly tricky. If you use too much, the paint takes weeks to dry and gets this weird sticky film on top. \n\nBut, uh, yes. The worry is definitely making it hard to just get through what I need to do every day. I just can't seem to focus.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like your worrying is causing you a lot of distress?\n\nPatient: Well, yes, it... it is. It's incredibly distressing. Like I mentioned a moment ago, it's just getting in the way of everything. I've had to slow down the whole retirement handoff at the clinic. I just sit there second-guessing every single decision, and before I know it, half the day is gone and I've accomplished nothing. It's exhausting. \n\nAnd it's not just work, either. Even my hobbies... I can't even enjoy them. Painting, gardening... they just don't feel manageable anymore. I can't quiet my mind enough to just sit and paint. I was actually looking at my easel this morning. I have this half-finished landscape I started three months ago. It's a view of the lighthouse up in Cape Elizabeth. We went there for our anniversary a few years back. The way the light hits the rocks at sunset there is just spectacular. There's a little seafood shack right near the lighthouse that serves the best lobster rolls I've ever had. They toast the bun in butter and just use a tiny bit of mayonnaise, which is really the only proper way to do it. Some places drown it in mayo and celery, and it just ruins the delicate flavor of the meat. Helen always gets the clam chowder, though. She says it reminds her of her grandmother's recipe. Her grandmother grew up in this tiny fishing village in Nova Scotia... \n\nBut, um... sorry. Yes. The distress. It's just very upsetting to feel like I can't even manage the things that used to bring me peace, or get through my work without losing hours to this... this endless worrying.\n\nDoctor: Has your worrying been affecting your relationships with family or friends?\n\nPatient: Well, no, I wouldn't say it's really affecting my relationships. I mean, Helen and I are fine, and I still see my friends. We actually had dinner with the Millers just last Friday. We went to that new Italian place downtown, the one that opened where the old hardware store used to be. You remember the hardware store? Old man Higgins ran it for, gosh, must have been forty years. You could go in there and find any obscure screw or washer you needed. He had these floor-to-ceiling wooden drawers, thousands of them, all labeled in this beautiful cursive handwriting. He used a fountain pen, I think. You don't see people using fountain pens much anymore. I actually bought one for myself when I graduated dental school, a Parker 51, classic design. They designed the nib so it wouldn't dry out when you were flying in unpressurized airplanes. \n\nBut, uh... no, the worrying hasn't really caused any problems with my family or friends. They've been very supportive, honestly.\n\nDoctor: Do you experience any physical symptoms like tremors, sweating, or rapid heartbeat when you're worrying?\n\nPatient: Well, no, I haven't really noticed anything like that. No shaking or sweating. My heart feels fine. Actually, Helen got me one of those smartwatches for my birthday a few months ago, and it tracks your heart rate right on the screen. It's that new model with the aerospace-grade titanium casing. \n\nTitanium is really a fascinating metal, you know. We use it constantly for implants at the clinic because it's completely biocompatible. The jawbone actually fuses right to it. It's a process called osseointegration. This Swedish researcher, Brånemark, discovered it completely by accident back in the fifties. He was studying blood flow in rabbits and put these little titanium optical chambers into their bones, and when he went to take them out later, they were permanently stuck! The bone had just grown right onto the metal. It completely revolutionized dentistry. Before implants became standard, patients were just stuck with these awful, ill-fitting dentures that would slide around if they even looked at an ear of corn or a tough steak. You have to use a special calibrated torque wrench to place the abutments on the implants so you don't strip the threading, and... \n\nOh, sorry. I got off track there. But, um, yes, the watch says my heart rate is perfectly normal all day. So no, no racing heart or tremors or anything like that when I'm worrying. Just the worry itself.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you can control your worrying when it starts?\n\nPatient: Well, I... I really try. I try to reason with myself, you know? Sort of the way I would with a nervous patient in the chair. We get a lot of those, people who are just terrified of the drill. I always tell my junior partners that bedside manner is half the job. You have to speak in this low, calm tone. I learned that from my mentor, Dr. Gable. He was a real old-school gentleman, always wore a bowtie to the office. He had this whole collection of them, silk ones, mostly from this little haberdashery in London. He went there every summer for a dental conference. London is a fascinating city for history. They found an entire Roman temple underneath a modern office building there once, the Temple of Mithras, I think it was called. They just stumbled on it while they were digging the foundation in the fifties. \n\nBut, uh, where was I? Oh, right, controlling it. No, it doesn't work when I try to calm myself down. One concern just immediately leads to the next one. Before I know it, I'm checking my emails again, reviewing patient notes for the third time, or just... asking the same questions over and over instead of just letting it go. It just spirals out of my hands.\n\nDoctor: Does your worrying follow you into most situations, or are there certain places or activities where it tends to fade away?\n\nPatient: Well, it pretty much follows me everywhere lately. I mean, my mind just keeps circling around all sorts of things. I worry constantly about whether my old patients are being looked after properly by the new partners. And then I start fretting over whether the practice handoff is financially sound. We used this accounting firm, Higgins and Price, to handle the valuation. Old man Price, well, his son actually, but the father started the firm back in the seventies. He was a real stickler for tax code. He used to play golf with my father-in-law at the country club. They had this beautiful 18-hole course, designed by some famous architect, I can't recall his name right now. But they had these incredibly tricky sand traps on the back nine. I always used a 56-degree wedge to get out of them. You really have to open the club face and slide it under the ball, sort of like how you use an elevator to extract a molar. You don't pull, you just gently wedge and lift. \n\nBut anyway, yeah, the worry just doesn't fade away. I even worry about whether I'll become completely useless once I'm fully retired. And honestly, the worry feels totally out of proportion, even to me. It's just always there, no matter where I am or what I'm doing.\n\nDoctor: Is there a particular domain or area in your life where your worrying seems to be most prominent?\n\nPatient: Well, no, not really one specific area. It’s sort of just... everything. Like, I’ll be worrying about the practice, and then I’ll look out the window and start worrying about the house. We have these copper gutters on the house. Helen insisted on copper because it gets that beautiful green patina over time, verdigris, they call it. It’s actually copper carbonate. We used to use copper amalgam in dentistry, well, mostly before my time, but they mixed copper with mercury for fillings. \n\nMercury is a really fascinating element, you know, being the only metal that’s liquid at room temperature. I remember breaking a glass thermometer when I was a boy and just playing with those little silver beads of mercury on the linoleum floor. You could push them together and they'd just merge into a bigger drop. My mother was absolutely furious when she caught me. She grew up during the Depression, so breaking anything useful was basically a cardinal sin in our house. She used to save the aluminum foil from baked potatoes, wash it off, and smooth it out to use again. And she kept a little tin can of bacon grease right on the stove... I used to love the smell of that kitchen in the mornings, especially on Sundays before church. \n\nBut, um... wait, I'm sorry, what were you asking me? Oh, right, a particular area of worry. No, I don't think so. It just sort of attaches itself to whatever is in front of me, really. There's no one specific thing.\n\nDoctor: When you worry, is it usually about things that might happen in the future?\n\nPatient: Well, I suppose so, yes. It's mostly about what's coming down the road. You know, what if this happens, what if that happens. Like with the retirement, what if the market takes a dive? I was just reading an article about inflation and how it affects fixed-income portfolios. It reminded me of the late seventies, early eighties, when inflation was just through the roof. I was still in dental school then. We used to go to this little diner near campus, and I swear the price of a cup of coffee went up every month. \n\nThe owner, a Greek fellow named Stavros, made the most incredible moussaka on Thursdays. He said the secret was in the bechamel sauce, getting the nutmeg just right. Nutmeg is actually quite interesting, historically speaking. Wars were fought over it in the Spice Islands. The Dutch traded the island of Manhattan to the British just to keep a monopoly on nutmeg. Can you imagine? New York City for a spice you sprinkle on eggnog. Eggnog always gives me terrible heartburn, though. I think it's the heavy cream. \n\nBut, uh... I'm sorry, I lost my train of thought. Future things. Yes, it's always the future. I rarely worry about the past, it's always what's going to happen tomorrow, or next month, or next year.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Well, yes, I... I really don't feel like I can stop it once it starts. I try to reason with myself, you know, the exact same way I would with a nervous patient in the chair. I used to have this one patient, Mrs. Gable, who was absolutely terrified of the drill. Even for a simple filling. We had to use nitrous oxide for her every single time. Nitrous is a fascinating gas, really. Discovered by Joseph Priestley back in the 1700s, but it was Horace Wells, a dentist actually, who first realized its potential for anesthesia. He saw a man injure his leg at a laughing gas exhibition and not feel any pain. Unfortunately, Wells's own public demonstration at Mass General was a disaster. The patient cried out, and they basically laughed Wells out of the room. Poor fellow ended up quite depressed. 

Depression was so misunderstood back then. They used to treat it with all sorts of bizarre things, like hydrotherapy, wrapping people in freezing cold wet sheets. My grandfather used to swear by cold showers every morning. He lived to be ninety-two, so maybe there was something to it. He was a carpenter, built half the houses in his neighborhood. He always said measure twice, cut once. 

But, uh, what was I saying? Oh, right, reasoning with myself. Yes, I try to talk myself down, but it just doesn't work. One concern just leads right into the next, and before I know it, I'm checking my emails at two in the morning, reviewing old patient notes, or just... asking my wife the same questions over and over again instead of just letting it go. It's definitely getting in the way of things.

But, um, yes, the watch says my heart rate is perfectly normal all day. So no, no racing heart or tremors or anything like that when I'm worrying. Just the worry itself.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you can control your worrying when it starts?\n\nPatient: Well, I... I really try. I try to reason with myself, you know? Sort of the way I would with a nervous patient in the chair. We get a lot of those, people who are just terrified of the drill. I always tell my junior partners that bedside manner is half the job. You have to speak in this low, calm tone. I learned that from my mentor, Dr. Gable. He was a real old-school gentleman, always wore a bowtie to the office. He had this whole collection of them, silk ones, mostly from this little haberdashery in London. He went there every summer for a dental conference. London is a fascinating city for history. They found an entire Roman temple underneath a modern office building there once, the Temple of Mithras, I think it was called. They just stumbled on it while they were digging the foundation in the fifties. \n\nBut, uh, where was I? Oh, right, controlling it. No, it doesn't work when I try to calm myself down. One concern just immediately leads to the next one. Before I know it, I'm checking my emails again, reviewing patient notes for the third time, or just... asking the same questions over and over instead of just letting it go. It just spirals out of my hands.\n\nDoctor: Does your worrying follow you into most situations, or are there certain places or activities where it tends to fade away?\n\nPatient: Well, it pretty much follows me everywhere lately. I mean, my mind just keeps circling around all sorts of things. I worry constantly about whether my old patients are being looked after properly by the new partners. And then I start fretting over whether the practice handoff is financially sound. We used this accounting firm, Higgins and Price, to handle the valuation. Old man Price, well, his son actually, but the father started the firm back in the seventies. He was a real stickler for tax code. He used to play golf with my father-in-law at the country club. They had this beautiful 18-hole course, designed by some famous architect, I can't recall his name right now. But they had these incredibly tricky sand traps on the back nine. I always used a 56-degree wedge to get out of them. You really have to open the club face and slide it under the ball, sort of like how you use an elevator to extract a molar. You don't pull, you just gently wedge and lift. \n\nBut anyway, yeah, the worry just doesn't fade away. I even worry about whether I'll become completely useless once I'm fully retired. And honestly, the worry feels totally out of proportion, even to me. It's just always there, no matter where I am or what I'm doing.\n\nDoctor: Is there a particular domain or area in your life where your worrying seems to be most prominent?\n\nPatient: Well, no, not really one specific area. It’s sort of just... everything. Like, I’ll be worrying about the practice, and then I’ll look out the window and start worrying about the house. We have these copper gutters on the house. Helen insisted on copper because it gets that beautiful green patina over time, verdigris, they call it. It’s actually copper carbonate. We used to use copper amalgam in dentistry, well, mostly before my time, but they mixed copper with mercury for fillings. \n\nMercury is a really fascinating element, you know, being the only metal that’s liquid at room temperature. I remember breaking a glass thermometer when I was a boy and just playing with those little silver beads of mercury on the linoleum floor. You could push them together and they'd just merge into a bigger drop. My mother was absolutely furious when she caught me. She grew up during the Depression, so breaking anything useful was basically a cardinal sin in our house. She used to save the aluminum foil from baked potatoes, wash it off, and smooth it out to use again. And she kept a little tin can of bacon grease right on the stove... I used to love the smell of that kitchen in the mornings, especially on Sundays before church. \n\nBut, um... wait, I'm sorry, what were you asking me? Oh, right, a particular area of worry. No, I don't think so. It just sort of attaches itself to whatever is in front of me, really. There's no one specific thing.\n\nDoctor: When you worry, is it usually about things that might happen in the future?\n\nPatient: Well, I suppose so, yes. It's mostly about what's coming down the road. You know, what if this happens, what if that happens. Like with the retirement, what if the market takes a dive? I was just reading an article about inflation and how it affects fixed-income portfolios. It reminded me of the late seventies, early eighties, when inflation was just through the roof. I was still in dental school then. We used to go to this little diner near campus, and I swear the price of a cup of coffee went up every month. \n\nThe owner, a Greek fellow named Stavros, made the most incredible moussaka on Thursdays. He said the secret was in the bechamel sauce, getting the nutmeg just right. Nutmeg is actually quite interesting, historically speaking. Wars were fought over it in the Spice Islands. The Dutch traded the island of Manhattan to the British just to keep a monopoly on nutmeg. Can you imagine? New York City for a spice you sprinkle on eggnog. Eggnog always gives me terrible heartburn, though. I think it's the heavy cream. \n\nBut, uh... I'm sorry, I lost my train of thought. Future things. Yes, it's always the future. I rarely worry about the past, it's always what's going to happen tomorrow, or next month, or next year.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your worrying is something you can't control, and it's interfering with your daily life more than it should?\n\nPatient: Well, yes, I... I really don't feel like I can stop it once it starts. I try to reason with myself, you know, the exact same way I would with a nervous patient in the chair. I used to have this one patient, Mrs. Gable, who was absolutely terrified of the drill. Even for a simple filling. We had to use nitrous oxide for her every single time. Nitrous is a fascinating gas, really. Discovered by Joseph Priestley back in the 1700s, but it was Horace Wells, a dentist actually, who first realized its potential for anesthesia. He saw a man injure his leg at a laughing gas exhibition and not feel any pain. Unfortunately, Wells's own public demonstration at Mass General was a disaster. The patient cried out, and they basically laughed Wells out of the room. Poor fellow ended up quite depressed. \n\nDepression was so misunderstood back then. They used to treat it with all sorts of bizarre things, like hydrotherapy, wrapping people in freezing cold wet sheets. My grandfather used to swear by cold showers every morning. He lived to be ninety-two, so maybe there was something to it. He was a carpenter, built half the houses in his neighborhood. He always said measure twice, cut once. \n\nBut, uh, what was I saying? Oh, right, reasoning with myself. Yes, I try to talk myself down, but it just doesn't work. One concern just leads right into the next, and before I know it, I'm checking my emails at two in the morning, reviewing old patient notes, or just... asking my wife the same questions over and over again instead of just letting it go. It's definitely getting in the way of things.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"

I remember breaking a glass thermometer when I was a boy and just playing with those little silver beads of mercury on the linoleum floor. You could push them together and they'd just merge into a bigger drop. My mother was absolutely furious when she caught me. She grew up during the Depression, so breaking anything useful was basically a cardinal sin in our house. She used to save the aluminum foil from baked potatoes, wash it off, and smooth it out to use again. And she kept a little tin can of bacon grease right on the stove... I used to love the smell of that kitchen in the mornings, especially on Sundays before church. \n\nBut, um... wait, I'm sorry, what were you asking me? Oh, right, a particular area of worry. No, I don't think so. It just sort of attaches itself to whatever is in front of me, really. There's no one specific thing.\n\nDoctor: When you worry, is it usually about things that might happen in the future?\n\nPatient: Well, I suppose so, yes. It's mostly about what's coming down the road. You know, what if this happens, what if that happens. Like with the retirement, what if the market takes a dive? I was just reading an article about inflation and how it affects fixed-income portfolios. It reminded me of the late seventies, early eighties, when inflation was just through the roof. I was still in dental school then. We used to go to this little diner near campus, and I swear the price of a cup of coffee went up every month. \n\nThe owner, a Greek fellow named Stavros, made the most incredible moussaka on Thursdays. He said the secret was in the bechamel sauce, getting the nutmeg just right. Nutmeg is actually quite interesting, historically speaking. Wars were fought over it in the Spice Islands. The Dutch traded the island of Manhattan to the British just to keep a monopoly on nutmeg. Can you imagine? New York City for a spice you sprinkle on eggnog. Eggnog always gives me terrible heartburn, though. I think it's the heavy cream. \n\nBut, uh... I'm sorry, I lost my train of thought. Future things. Yes, it's always the future. I rarely worry about the past, it's always what's going to happen tomorrow, or next month, or next year.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your worrying is something you can't control, and it's interfering with your daily life more than it should?\n\nPatient: Well, yes, I... I really don't feel like I can stop it once it starts. I try to reason with myself, you know, the exact same way I would with a nervous patient in the chair. I used to have this one patient, Mrs. Gable, who was absolutely terrified of the drill. Even for a simple filling. We had to use nitrous oxide for her every single time. Nitrous is a fascinating gas, really. Discovered by Joseph Priestley back in the 1700s, but it was Horace Wells, a dentist actually, who first realized its potential for anesthesia. He saw a man injure his leg at a laughing gas exhibition and not feel any pain. Unfortunately, Wells's own public demonstration at Mass General was a disaster. The patient cried out, and they basically laughed Wells out of the room. Poor fellow ended up quite depressed. \n\nDepression was so misunderstood back then. They used to treat it with all sorts of bizarre things, like hydrotherapy, wrapping people in freezing cold wet sheets. My grandfather used to swear by cold showers every morning. He lived to be ninety-two, so maybe there was something to it. He was a carpenter, built half the houses in his neighborhood. He always said measure twice, cut once. \n\nBut, uh, what was I saying? Oh, right, reasoning with myself. Yes, I try to talk myself down, but it just doesn't work. One concern just leads right into the next, and before I know it, I'm checking my emails at two in the morning, reviewing old patient notes, or just... asking my wife the same questions over and over again instead of just letting it go. It's definitely getting in the way of things.\n\n[Last Question]\nIs your worrying usually about everyday, minor things or are they more significant life issues?\n\nRules:\n- Return exactly ONE item. Well, it's... it's really a mix of both, I suppose. I mean, I worry about the big things, like the practice and retirement, but then I'll get completely caught up in the minor stuff, too. Like, the other day I spent an hour worrying about whether I had left the sprinkler system on. We just had a new one installed, a smart system that connects to the Wi-Fi. It's supposed to adjust itself based on the weather forecast. 

Wi-Fi is just incredible when you think about it. The fact that all this information is just bouncing around in the air, invisible. Hedy Lamarr actually helped invent the technology that led to Wi-Fi. The actress, yes! She and a composer named George Antheil developed a frequency-hopping system during World War II to keep torpedoes from being jammed. They used a player piano mechanism to synchronize the signals. I've always loved player pianos. My great-aunt had one in her parlor. You'd pump the pedals and the paper roll would turn, and the keys would just play themselves like a ghost was sitting there. She had all these old ragtime tunes, Scott Joplin mostly. "The Maple Leaf Rag" was her favorite. Ragtime has such a unique syncopation, it really set the stage for jazz later on. 

But, um... sorry, I'm doing it again. Everyday things. Yes, it can be the smallest thing, like the sprinklers, or whether I paid the electric bill on time, even though it's been on autopay for five years. It's just... anything, really.

You could push them together and they'd just merge into a bigger drop. My mother was absolutely furious when she caught me. She grew up during the Depression, so breaking anything useful was basically a cardinal sin in our house. She used to save the aluminum foil from baked potatoes, wash it off, and smooth it out to use again. And she kept a little tin can of bacon grease right on the stove... I used to love the smell of that kitchen in the mornings, especially on Sundays before church. \n\nBut, um... wait, I'm sorry, what were you asking me? Oh, right, a particular area of worry. No, I don't think so. It just sort of attaches itself to whatever is in front of me, really. There's no one specific thing.\n\nDoctor: When you worry, is it usually about things that might happen in the future?\n\nPatient: Well, I suppose so, yes. It's mostly about what's coming down the road. You know, what if this happens, what if that happens. Like with the retirement, what if the market takes a dive? I was just reading an article about inflation and how it affects fixed-income portfolios. It reminded me of the late seventies, early eighties, when inflation was just through the roof. I was still in dental school then. We used to go to this little diner near campus, and I swear the price of a cup of coffee went up every month. \n\nThe owner, a Greek fellow named Stavros, made the most incredible moussaka on Thursdays. He said the secret was in the bechamel sauce, getting the nutmeg just right. Nutmeg is actually quite interesting, historically speaking. Wars were fought over it in the Spice Islands. The Dutch traded the island of Manhattan to the British just to keep a monopoly on nutmeg. Can you imagine? New York City for a spice you sprinkle on eggnog. Eggnog always gives me terrible heartburn, though. I think it's the heavy cream. \n\nBut, uh... I'm sorry, I lost my train of thought. Future things. Yes, it's always the future. I rarely worry about the past, it's always what's going to happen tomorrow, or next month, or next year.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your worrying is something you can't control, and it's interfering with your daily life more than it should?\n\nPatient: Well, yes, I... I really don't feel like I can stop it once it starts. I try to reason with myself, you know, the exact same way I would with a nervous patient in the chair. I used to have this one patient, Mrs. Gable, who was absolutely terrified of the drill. Even for a simple filling. We had to use nitrous oxide for her every single time. Nitrous is a fascinating gas, really. Discovered by Joseph Priestley back in the 1700s, but it was Horace Wells, a dentist actually, who first realized its potential for anesthesia. He saw a man injure his leg at a laughing gas exhibition and not feel any pain. Unfortunately, Wells's own public demonstration at Mass General was a disaster. The patient cried out, and they basically laughed Wells out of the room. Poor fellow ended up quite depressed. \n\nDepression was so misunderstood back then. They used to treat it with all sorts of bizarre things, like hydrotherapy, wrapping people in freezing cold wet sheets. My grandfather used to swear by cold showers every morning. He lived to be ninety-two, so maybe there was something to it. He was a carpenter, built half the houses in his neighborhood. He always said measure twice, cut once. \n\nBut, uh, what was I saying? Oh, right, reasoning with myself. Yes, I try to talk myself down, but it just doesn't work. One concern just leads right into the next, and before I know it, I'm checking my emails at two in the morning, reviewing old patient notes, or just... asking my wife the same questions over and over again instead of just letting it go. It's definitely getting in the way of things.\n\nDoctor: Is your worrying usually about everyday, minor things or are they more significant life issues?\n\nPatient: Well, it's... it's really a mix of both, I suppose. I mean, I worry about the big things, like the practice and retirement, but then I'll get completely caught up in the minor stuff, too. Like, the other day I spent an hour worrying about whether I had left the sprinkler system on. We just had a new one installed, a smart system that connects to the Wi-Fi. It's supposed to adjust itself based on the weather forecast. \n\nWi-Fi is just incredible when you think about it. The fact that all this information is just bouncing around in the air, invisible. Hedy Lamarr actually helped invent the technology that led to Wi-Fi. The actress, yes! She and a composer named George Antheil developed a frequency-hopping system during World War II to keep torpedoes from being jammed. They used a player piano mechanism to synchronize the signals. I've always loved player pianos. My great-aunt had one in her parlor. You'd pump the pedals and the paper roll would turn, and the keys would just play themselves like a ghost was sitting there. She had all these old ragtime tunes, Scott Joplin mostly. \"The Maple Leaf Rag\" was her favorite. Ragtime has such a unique syncopation, it really set the stage for jazz later on. \n\nBut, um... sorry, I'm doing it again. Everyday things. Yes, it can be the smallest thing, like the sprinklers, or whether I paid the electric bill on time, even though it's been on autopay for five years. It's just... anything, really.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"

Eggnog always gives me terrible heartburn, though. I think it's the heavy cream. \n\nBut, uh... I'm sorry, I lost my train of thought. Future things. Yes, it's always the future. I rarely worry about the past, it's always what's going to happen tomorrow, or next month, or next year.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your worrying is something you can't control, and it's interfering with your daily life more than it should?\n\nPatient: Well, yes, I... I really don't feel like I can stop it once it starts. I try to reason with myself, you know, the exact same way I would with a nervous patient in the chair. I used to have this one patient, Mrs. Gable, who was absolutely terrified of the drill. Even for a simple filling. We had to use nitrous oxide for her every single time. Nitrous is a fascinating gas, really. Discovered by Joseph Priestley back in the 1700s, but it was Horace Wells, a dentist actually, who first realized its potential for anesthesia. He saw a man injure his leg at a laughing gas exhibition and not feel any pain. Unfortunately, Wells's own public demonstration at Mass General was a disaster. The patient cried out, and they basically laughed Wells out of the room. Poor fellow ended up quite depressed. \n\nDepression was so misunderstood back then. They used to treat it with all sorts of bizarre things, like hydrotherapy, wrapping people in freezing cold wet sheets. My grandfather used to swear by cold showers every morning. He lived to be ninety-two, so maybe there was something to it. He was a carpenter, built half the houses in his neighborhood. He always said measure twice, cut once. \n\nBut, uh, what was I saying? Oh, right, reasoning with myself. Yes, I try to talk myself down, but it just doesn't work. One concern just leads right into the next, and before I know it, I'm checking my emails at two in the morning, reviewing old patient notes, or just... asking my wife the same questions over and over again instead of just letting it go. It's definitely getting in the way of things.\n\nDoctor: Is your worrying usually about everyday, minor things or are they more significant life issues?\n\nPatient: Well, it's... it's really a mix of both, I suppose. I mean, I worry about the big things, like the practice and retirement, but then I'll get completely caught up in the minor stuff, too. Like, the other day I spent an hour worrying about whether I had left the sprinkler system on. We just had a new one installed, a smart system that connects to the Wi-Fi. It's supposed to adjust itself based on the weather forecast. \n\nWi-Fi is just incredible when you think about it. The fact that all this information is just bouncing around in the air, invisible. Hedy Lamarr actually helped invent the technology that led to Wi-Fi. The actress, yes! She and a composer named George Antheil developed a frequency-hopping system during World War II to keep torpedoes from being jammed. They used a player piano mechanism to synchronize the signals. I've always loved player pianos. My great-aunt had one in her parlor. You'd pump the pedals and the paper roll would turn, and the keys would just play themselves like a ghost was sitting there. She had all these old ragtime tunes, Scott Joplin mostly. \"The Maple Leaf Rag\" was her favorite. Ragtime has such a unique syncopation, it really set the stage for jazz later on. \n\nBut, um... sorry, I'm doing it again. Everyday things. Yes, it can be the smallest thing, like the sprinklers, or whether I paid the electric bill on time, even though it's been on autopay for five years. It's just... anything, really.\n\n[Last Question]\nDo you often feel restless or on edge when you're worrying?\n\nRules:\n- Return exactly ONE item. If the question touches several topics, return the one it is\n  most directly about — never more than one.\n- Copy `key`, `name` and `description` exactly as they appear in [Symptom Profiles].\n- If your profile does not cover what the doctor asked, return the text \"None\" for `key`,\n  `name` and `description`, and say why in `reason`. Restless? On edge? I... I'm not really sure, to be honest. I haven't specifically noticed feeling that way. I sit for most of the day, you see, so maybe I just don't pick up on being physically restless. Sitting on that little operator stool all day... you know, dental ergonomics is a massive field now. Back in the early days, dentists just stood. It absolutely ruined their spines. 

The first real dental chair with an adjustable headrest was actually invented by a man named Josiah Flagg, right around the late 1700s. He was a fascinating fellow, actually trained by Paul Revere. Most people just know Revere for the midnight ride, but he was a silversmith who did quite a bit of dentistry on the side. He even identified the body of Dr. Joseph Warren after the Battle of Bunker Hill by a silver dental bridge he had made for him. It was the very first recorded case of forensic dentistry in America! Warren was a major figure, he's actually the one who sent Revere on that ride in the first place. Listen my children and you shall hear, and all that. Longfellow's poem is wildly historically inaccurate, of course. William Dawes and Samuel Prescott did most of the actual riding, but Revere just had a better name for poetry. Dawes doesn't rhyme with much. Claws? Flaws? 

But, oh, excuse me. I'm drifting again. You were asking about feeling on edge. Honestly, I couldn't tell you. When the worrying starts, I just get so stuck in my own head that I don't really pay attention to what my body is doing. So I'm really not sure if I get restless or not.

The Dutch traded the island of Manhattan to the British just to keep a monopoly on nutmeg. Can you imagine? New York City for a spice you sprinkle on eggnog. Eggnog always gives me terrible heartburn, though. I think it's the heavy cream. \n\nBut, uh... I'm sorry, I lost my train of thought. Future things. Yes, it's always the future. I rarely worry about the past, it's always what's going to happen tomorrow, or next month, or next year.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your worrying is something you can't control, and it's interfering with your daily life more than it should?\n\nPatient: Well, yes, I... I really don't feel like I can stop it once it starts. I try to reason with myself, you know, the exact same way I would with a nervous patient in the chair. I used to have this one patient, Mrs. Gable, who was absolutely terrified of the drill. Even for a simple filling. We had to use nitrous oxide for her every single time. Nitrous is a fascinating gas, really. Discovered by Joseph Priestley back in the 1700s, but it was Horace Wells, a dentist actually, who first realized its potential for anesthesia. He saw a man injure his leg at a laughing gas exhibition and not feel any pain. Unfortunately, Wells's own public demonstration at Mass General was a disaster. The patient cried out, and they basically laughed Wells out of the room. Poor fellow ended up quite depressed. \n\nDepression was so misunderstood back then. They used to treat it with all sorts of bizarre things, like hydrotherapy, wrapping people in freezing cold wet sheets. My grandfather used to swear by cold showers every morning. He lived to be ninety-two, so maybe there was something to it. He was a carpenter, built half the houses in his neighborhood. He always said measure twice, cut once. \n\nBut, uh, what was I saying? Oh, right, reasoning with myself. Yes, I try to talk myself down, but it just doesn't work. One concern just leads right into the next, and before I know it, I'm checking my emails at two in the morning, reviewing old patient notes, or just... asking my wife the same questions over and over again instead of just letting it go. It's definitely getting in the way of things.\n\nDoctor: Is your worrying usually about everyday, minor things or are they more significant life issues?\n\nPatient: Well, it's... it's really a mix of both, I suppose. I mean, I worry about the big things, like the practice and retirement, but then I'll get completely caught up in the minor stuff, too. Like, the other day I spent an hour worrying about whether I had left the sprinkler system on. We just had a new one installed, a smart system that connects to the Wi-Fi. It's supposed to adjust itself based on the weather forecast. \n\nWi-Fi is just incredible when you think about it. The fact that all this information is just bouncing around in the air, invisible. Hedy Lamarr actually helped invent the technology that led to Wi-Fi. The actress, yes! She and a composer named George Antheil developed a frequency-hopping system during World War II to keep torpedoes from being jammed. They used a player piano mechanism to synchronize the signals. I've always loved player pianos. My great-aunt had one in her parlor. You'd pump the pedals and the paper roll would turn, and the keys would just play themselves like a ghost was sitting there. She had all these old ragtime tunes, Scott Joplin mostly. \"The Maple Leaf Rag\" was her favorite. Ragtime has such a unique syncopation, it really set the stage for jazz later on. \n\nBut, um... sorry, I'm doing it again. Everyday things. Yes, it can be the smallest thing, like the sprinklers, or whether I paid the electric bill on time, even though it's been on autopay for five years. It's just... anything, really.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel restless or on edge when you're worrying?\n\nPatient: Restless? On edge? I... I'm not really sure, to be honest. I haven't specifically noticed feeling that way. I sit for most of the day, you see, so maybe I just don't pick up on being physically restless. Sitting on that little operator stool all day... you know, dental ergonomics is a massive field now. Back in the early days, dentists just stood. It absolutely ruined their spines. \n\nThe first real dental chair with an adjustable headrest was actually invented by a man named Josiah Flagg, right around the late 1700s. He was a fascinating fellow, actually trained by Paul Revere. Most people just know Revere for the midnight ride, but he was a silversmith who did quite a bit of dentistry on the side. He even identified the body of Dr. Joseph Warren after the Battle of Bunker Hill by a silver dental bridge he had made for him. It was the very first recorded case of forensic dentistry in America! Warren was a major figure, he's actually the one who sent Revere on that ride in the first place. Listen my children and you shall hear, and all that. Longfellow's poem is wildly historically inaccurate, of course. William Dawes and Samuel Prescott did most of the actual riding, but Revere just had a better name for poetry. Dawes doesn't rhyme with much. Claws? Flaws? \n\nBut, oh, excuse me. I'm drifting again. You were asking about feeling on edge. Honestly, I couldn't tell you. When the worrying starts, I just get so stuck in my own head that I don't really pay attention to what my body is doing. So I'm really not sure if I get restless or not.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. chief_complaint_symptom_code: S059\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Do you think your worrying is something you can't control, and it's interfering with your daily life more than it should?\n\nPatient: Well, yes, I... I really don't feel like I can stop it once it starts. I try to reason with myself, you know, the exact same way I would with a nervous patient in the chair. I used to have this one patient, Mrs. Gable, who was absolutely terrified of the drill. Even for a simple filling. We had to use nitrous oxide for her every single time. Nitrous is a fascinating gas, really. Discovered by Joseph Priestley back in the 1700s, but it was Horace Wells, a dentist actually, who first realized its potential for anesthesia. He saw a man injure his leg at a laughing gas exhibition and not feel any pain. Unfortunately, Wells's own public demonstration at Mass General was a disaster. The patient cried out, and they basically laughed Wells out of the room. Poor fellow ended up quite depressed. \n\nDepression was so misunderstood back then. They used to treat it with all sorts of bizarre things, like hydrotherapy, wrapping people in freezing cold wet sheets. My grandfather used to swear by cold showers every morning. He lived to be ninety-two, so maybe there was something to it. He was a carpenter, built half the houses in his neighborhood. He always said measure twice, cut once. \n\nBut, uh, what was I saying? Oh, right, reasoning with myself. Yes, I try to talk myself down, but it just doesn't work. One concern just leads right into the next, and before I know it, I'm checking my emails at two in the morning, reviewing old patient notes, or just... asking my wife the same questions over and over again instead of just letting it go. It's definitely getting in the way of things.\n\nDoctor: Is your worrying usually about everyday, minor things or are they more significant life issues?\n\nPatient: Well, it's... it's really a mix of both, I suppose. I mean, I worry about the big things, like the practice and retirement, but then I'll get completely caught up in the minor stuff, too. Like, the other day I spent an hour worrying about whether I had left the sprinkler system on. We just had a new one installed, a smart system that connects to the Wi-Fi. It's supposed to adjust itself based on the weather forecast. \n\nWi-Fi is just incredible when you think about it. The fact that all this information is just bouncing around in the air, invisible. Hedy Lamarr actually helped invent the technology that led to Wi-Fi. The actress, yes! She and a composer named George Antheil developed a frequency-hopping system during World War II to keep torpedoes from being jammed. They used a player piano mechanism to synchronize the signals. I've always loved player pianos. My great-aunt had one in her parlor. You'd pump the pedals and the paper roll would turn, and the keys would just play themselves like a ghost was sitting there. She had all these old ragtime tunes, Scott Joplin mostly. \"The Maple Leaf Rag\" was her favorite. Ragtime has such a unique syncopation, it really set the stage for jazz later on. \n\nBut, um... sorry, I'm doing it again. Everyday things. It's just... anything, really.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel restless or on edge when you're worrying?\n\nPatient: Restless? On edge? I... I'm not really sure, to be honest. I haven't specifically noticed feeling that way. I sit for most of the day, you see, so maybe I just don't pick up on being physically restless. Sitting on that little operator stool all day... you know, dental ergonomics is a massive field now. Back in the early days, dentists just stood. It absolutely ruined their spines. \n\nThe first real dental chair with an adjustable headrest was actually invented by a man named Josiah Flagg, right around the late 1700s. He was a fascinating fellow, actually trained by Paul Revere. Most people just know Revere for the midnight ride, but he was a silversmith who did quite a bit of dentistry on the side. He even identified the body of Dr. Joseph Warren after the Battle of Bunker Hill by a silver dental bridge he had made for him. It was the very first recorded case of forensic dentistry in America! Dawes doesn't rhyme with much. Claws? Flaws? \n\nBut, oh, excuse me. I'm drifting again. You were asking about feeling on edge. Honestly, I couldn't tell you. When the worrying starts, I just get so stuck in my own head that I don't really pay attention to what my body is doing. So I'm really not sure if I get restless or not.\n\n[Last Question]\nWhen you worry, are you usually worried about things that might actually happen or things that are unlikely to happen?\n\nRules:\n- Return exactly ONE item. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Well, I suppose they are things that *could* happen, but... my mind just keeps circling around all sorts of things, and the worry feels completely out of proportion, even to me. I'll lay awake thinking about whether my old patients are being looked after properly by the new associates. And then I start agonizing over whether the practice handoff is actually financially sound, or if I'm just going to become completely useless once I'm fully retired. 

Retirement is a strange concept, isn't it? It didn't really exist as a widespread institution until the late 19th century. Chancellor Otto von Bismarck in Germany actually introduced the first state-sponsored retirement system in 1889. The retirement age was originally set at 70, which was quite old back then! Bismarck was a fascinating figure, the "Iron Chancellor." He unified Germany through a series of wars. The Franco-Prussian War, for instance, completely shifted the balance of power in Europe. It's amazing how much history turns on a single conflict. My father actually fought in World War II, in the Pacific theater. He brought back this incredible Japanese ceremonial sword, a katana. The craftsmanship on those blades is astonishing. They fold the steel dozens of times to remove impurities. A good swordsmith was revered almost like a priest in feudal Japan. 

But, uh, I'm sorry, I've wandered off again. You were asking if the things I worry about are unlikely. Like I said, they *could* happen—the practice could struggle, I suppose—but I know the amount of time I spend obsessing over these things is just... it's just too much. I can't seem to shut it off.

And honestly, the worry feels totally out of proportion, even to me. It's just always there, no matter where I am or what I'm doing.\n\nDoctor: Is there a particular domain or area in your life where your worrying seems to be most prominent?\n\nPatient: Well, no, not really one specific area. It’s sort of just... everything. Like, I’ll be worrying about the practice, and then I’ll look out the window and start worrying about the house. We have these copper gutters on the house. Helen insisted on copper because it gets that beautiful green patina over time, verdigris, they call it. It’s actually copper carbonate. We used to use copper amalgam in dentistry, well, mostly before my time, but they mixed copper with mercury for fillings. \n\nMercury is a really fascinating element, you know, being the only metal that’s liquid at room temperature. I remember breaking a glass thermometer when I was a boy and just playing with those little silver beads of mercury on the linoleum floor. You could push them together and they'd just merge into a bigger drop. My mother was absolutely furious when she caught me. She grew up during the Depression, so breaking anything useful was basically a cardinal sin in our house. She used to save the aluminum foil from baked potatoes, wash it off, and smooth it out to use again. And she kept a little tin can of bacon grease right on the stove... I used to love the smell of that kitchen in the mornings, especially on Sundays before church. \n\nBut, um... wait, I'm sorry, what were you asking me? Oh, right, a particular area of worry. No, I don't think so. It just sort of attaches itself to whatever is in front of me, really. There's no one specific thing.\n\nDoctor: When you worry, is it usually about things that might happen in the future?\n\nPatient: Well, I suppose so, yes. It's mostly about what's coming down the road. You know, what if this happens, what if that happens. Like with the retirement, what if the market takes a dive? I was just reading an article about inflation and how it affects fixed-income portfolios. It reminded me of the late seventies, early eighties, when inflation was just through the roof. I was still in dental school then. We used to go to this little diner near campus, and I swear the price of a cup of coffee went up every month. \n\nThe owner, a Greek fellow named Stavros, made the most incredible moussaka on Thursdays. He said the secret was in the bechamel sauce, getting the nutmeg just right. Nutmeg is actually quite interesting, historically speaking. Wars were fought over it in the Spice Islands. The Dutch traded the island of Manhattan to the British just to keep a monopoly on nutmeg. Can you imagine? New York City for a spice you sprinkle on eggnog. Eggnog always gives me terrible heartburn, though. I think it's the heavy cream. \n\nBut, uh... I'm sorry, I lost my train of thought. Future things. Yes, it's always the future. I rarely worry about the past, it's always what's going to happen tomorrow, or next month, or next year.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your worrying is something you can't control, and it's interfering with your daily life more than it should?\n\nPatient: Well, yes, I... I really don't feel like I can stop it once it starts. I try to reason with myself, you know, the exact same way I would with a nervous patient in the chair. I used to have this one patient, Mrs. Gable, who was absolutely terrified of the drill. Even for a simple filling. We had to use nitrous oxide for her every single time. Nitrous is a fascinating gas, really. Discovered by Joseph Priestley back in the 1700s, but it was Horace Wells, a dentist actually, who first realized its potential for anesthesia. He saw a man injure his leg at a laughing gas exhibition and not feel any pain. Unfortunately, Wells's own public demonstration at Mass General was a disaster. The patient cried out, and they basically laughed Wells out of the room. Poor fellow ended up quite depressed. \n\nDepression was so misunderstood back then. They used to treat it with all sorts of bizarre things, like hydrotherapy, wrapping people in freezing cold wet sheets. My grandfather used to swear by cold showers every morning. He lived to be ninety-two, so maybe there was something to it. He was a carpenter, built half the houses in his neighborhood. He always said measure twice, cut once. \n\nBut, uh, what was I saying? Oh, right, reasoning with myself. Yes, I try to talk myself down, but it just doesn't work. One concern just leads right into the next, and before I know it, I'm checking my emails at two in the morning, reviewing old patient notes, or just... asking my wife the same questions over and over again instead of just letting it go. It's definitely getting in the way of things.\n\nDoctor: Is your worrying usually about everyday, minor things or are they more significant life issues?\n\nPatient: Well, it's... it's really a mix of both, I suppose. I mean, I worry about the big things, like the practice and retirement, but then I'll get completely caught up in the minor stuff, too. Like, the other day I spent an hour worrying about whether I had left the sprinkler system on. We just had a new one installed, a smart system that connects to the Wi-Fi. It's supposed to adjust itself based on the weather forecast. \n\nWi-Fi is just incredible when you think about it. The fact that all this information is just bouncing around in the air, invisible. Hedy Lamarr actually helped invent the technology that led to Wi-Fi. The actress, yes! She and a composer named George Antheil developed a frequency-hopping system during World War II to keep torpedoes from being jammed. They used a player piano mechanism to synchronize the signals. I've always loved player pianos. My great-aunt had one in her parlor. You'd pump the pedals and the paper roll would turn, and the keys would just play themselves like a ghost was sitting there. She had all these old ragtime tunes, Scott Joplin mostly. \"The Maple Leaf Rag\" was her favorite. Ragtime has such a unique syncopation, it really set the stage for jazz later on. \n\nBut, um... sorry, I'm doing it again. Everyday things. Yes, it can be the smallest thing, like the sprinklers, or whether I paid the electric bill on time, even though it's been on autopay for five years. It's just... anything, really.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel restless or on edge when you're worrying?\n\nPatient: Restless? On edge? I... I'm not really sure, to be honest. I haven't specifically noticed feeling that way. I sit for most of the day, you see, so maybe I just don't pick up on being physically restless. Sitting on that little operator stool all day... you know, dental ergonomics is a massive field now. Back in the early days, dentists just stood. It absolutely ruined their spines. \n\nThe first real dental chair with an adjustable headrest was actually invented by a man named Josiah Flagg, right around the late 1700s. He was a fascinating fellow, actually trained by Paul Revere. Most people just know Revere for the midnight ride, but he was a silversmith who did quite a bit of dentistry on the side. He even identified the body of Dr. Joseph Warren after the Battle of Bunker Hill by a silver dental bridge he had made for him. It was the very first recorded case of forensic dentistry in America! Warren was a major figure, he's actually the one who sent Revere on that ride in the first place. Listen my children and you shall hear, and all that. Longfellow's poem is wildly historically inaccurate, of course. William Dawes and Samuel Prescott did most of the actual riding, but Revere just had a better name for poetry. Dawes doesn't rhyme with much. Claws? Flaws? \n\nBut, oh, excuse me. I'm drifting again. You were asking about feeling on edge. Honestly, I couldn't tell you. When the worrying starts, I just get so stuck in my own head that I don't really pay attention to what my body is doing. So I'm really not sure if I get restless or not.\n\nDoctor: When you worry, are you usually worried about things that might actually happen or things that are unlikely to happen?\n\nPatient: Well, I suppose they are things that *could* happen, but... my mind just keeps circling around all sorts of things, and the worry feels completely out of proportion, even to me. I'll lay awake thinking about whether my old patients are being looked after properly by the new associates. And then I start agonizing over whether the practice handoff is actually financially sound, or if I'm just going to become completely useless once I'm fully retired. \n\nRetirement is a strange concept, isn't it? It didn't really exist as a widespread institution until the late 19th century. Chancellor Otto von Bismarck in Germany actually introduced the first state-sponsored retirement system in 1889. The retirement age was originally set at 70, which was quite old back then! Bismarck was a fascinating figure, the \"Iron Chancellor.\" He unified Germany through a series of wars. The Franco-Prussian War, for instance, completely shifted the balance of power in Europe. It's amazing how much history turns on a single conflict. My father actually fought in World War II, in the Pacific theater. He brought back this incredible Japanese ceremonial sword, a katana. The craftsmanship on those blades is astonishing. They fold the steel dozens of times to remove impurities. A good swordsmith was revered almost like a priest in feudal Japan. \n\nBut, uh, I'm sorry, I've wandered off again. You were asking if the things I worry about are unlikely. Like I said, they *could* happen—the practice could struggle, I suppose—but I know the amount of time I spend obsessing over these things is just... it's just too much. I can't seem to shut it off.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. Well, yes, actually. I've had to slow down quite a bit, especially with the practice handoff. I keep postponing parts of the transition because I'm just second-guessing every little decision, and I end up losing so much time at the clinic just... worrying. And it's bleeding into my personal time, too. My painting and gardening, they just don't feel as manageable as they used to. 

Gardening is usually such a peaceful thing, you know? I've been trying to cultivate these David Austin roses. They're English roses, bred to have the form and fragrance of old roses but with the repeat-flowering of modern ones. David Austin started breeding them in the 1950s. He was just a teenager when he got interested in them after reading a book by A.E. Bunyard. Bunyard was an interesting fellow, a pomologist—an expert in apples, mostly. The history of the apple is fascinating. They originated in Central Asia, in Kazakhstan. The wild ancestor of the domestic apple is still growing there in these massive forests. The capital city of Kazakhstan, Almaty, literally translates to "father of apples." I've always wanted to visit Central Asia. The Silk Road, Samarkand, all that history with Genghis Khan and the Mongol Empire. The Mongols had this incredible postal system called the Yam, which used a series of relay stations to transmit messages across the empire at astonishing speeds. Marco Polo wrote about it when he traveled there. 

But, ah, I'm sorry, I've done it again, haven't I? You were asking about things I've been putting off. Yes, the practice transition is really stalling, and I just can't seem to find the energy or focus for my painting or the garden anymore. The worrying just seems to take up all the room.

I mean, back when I started, we didn't need all these flashing lights and gadgets to do a simple crown prep. Just a steady hand.
